  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I just haven’t been myself since things changed at work. I work in inspection, and I’ve been doing my procedures a certain way for years—carefully, the way I was trained, and honestly I’ve always felt pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and she’s been picking apart the way I do things, the inspection procedures, the little calls I make day to day, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore.\n\nI come home and I’m still wound up from meetings or comments she made, and I’ll be replaying them in my head over and over—what I said, what I should’ve said, whether I looked incompetent. Then I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, which isn’t fair to him. At night I lie there going back through work in my head instead of sleeping, and then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice. It’s affecting me more than I want to admit, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work interactions even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s honestly one of the biggest things bothering me. It’s like I’ll be home, trying to do something normal, and then all of a sudden I’m right back in one of those meetings with the new operations manager, hearing the way she questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I’ll go over what she said, what I said back, whether I sounded defensive, whether maybe I missed something, and I can’t seem to just… put it down.\n\nEven when I’m not trying to think about it, it kind of forces its way in. I come home still wound up from work, and then I’ll be short with my husband because my mind is still stuck there. At night especially, I’ll lie awake replaying the comments and the decisions I made, and then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that used to feel simple to me. It’s like work follows me home now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m refusing to do my job, because I still care about doing it right, but I’ve definitely pulled back in ways that are starting to show.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If there’s an internal meeting where I know the procedures might come up, or where she might question how I made a call, I find myself trying to stay quiet or even avoiding being there if I can. And that’s not really like me. I used to be pretty dependable about showing up, giving my input, getting things done on time. But lately I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I get caught up hesitating or rechecking myself, and then I fall behind.\n\nMy work record has taken a hit, which is embarrassing to say out loud. I’ve always thought of myself as steady and reliable, and now I feel like I’m becoming the person who’s late with things or not fully present when I’m expected to be.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, honestly. At home, I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. My husband and I have been arguing more, partly because I come home already tense and then I’m not really there with him. So yes, I guess I am avoiding some situations at work, and it’s spilling over more than I wanted it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since these issues at work started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t say with absolute certainty, but my clothes seem to fit about the same.\n\nMy appetite… maybe some days I’m less interested in food just because I’m preoccupied, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a clear change like I’m not eating or I’m eating a lot more. Nothing has really stood out to me the way the work stress has.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing anxiety or feeling on edge in situations that have nothing to do with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s tied back to work, if I’m being honest. I mean, I don’t walk into the grocery store or sit at home for no reason and suddenly feel anxious out of nowhere. It’s more like… work has gotten under my skin so much that it comes with me everywhere.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave it there. I’ll be home, technically away from work, but I’m still wound up like I’m about to be questioned again. Then I snap at my husband over something small, or I’m lying awake replaying a meeting and thinking through everything I said. So it may look like I’m on edge at home, but in my head it’s still connected to those work interactions.\n\nAnd then the next day I go back in already tense, and I start second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without much trouble. So… not really separate from work, no. It spreads into other places, but the source still feels like work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the time since these work issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it sad or depressed most of the time. I mean… I’ve definitely been upset and discouraged about what’s been happening at work, and there are days when I feel pretty low after something goes badly or I feel criticized. But it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day, every day.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated, or embarrassed, or kind of worn down by it. Then when I’m away from the situation, I can still get pulled back into thinking about it, but I wouldn’t say I’m just sad all the time. I guess I’m having a hard time separating being stressed and disappointed from being depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop thinking about these work interactions, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because I feel like I keep trying to shut it off and I just can’t.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started going over my inspection procedures and questioning things I’ve been doing for years, it’s like it follows me home. I’ll tell myself, okay, you’re not at work now, stop thinking about it, but then I’m standing in the kitchen or trying to relax and my mind is right back in the meeting. I replay what was said, what I should have said, whether I sounded defensive, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then I get wound up all over again. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, not because of him really, but because I’m still carrying that stress around. Then at night I’m lying there going through the same conversations in my head, almost like I’m preparing to be criticized again. By the time I go back in the next day, I’m already tense, and then I start second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without much trouble. So yes, I do try to stop thinking about it, but it feels like my brain just keeps dragging me back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for the way things are going at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I blame myself exactly. I do go over it in my head a lot and wonder if I should have handled certain conversations differently, or if I should have explained my process better, or maybe not sounded so defensive. So there’s some of that, like questioning myself.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure that’s the right word. I don’t feel like I’ve done something wrong on purpose or that I caused the whole situation. It’s more that when someone keeps challenging the way you do your job, especially work you’ve done for years, it makes you start wondering, “Am I missing something? Did I get too comfortable? Should I have seen this coming?” And then I can get stuck picking apart my own part in it.\n\nSo yes, I second-guess myself, but I don’t think I’m walking around feeling guilty all the time. It’s more confusion and self-doubt than guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or impairment in your social or occupational functioning as a result of your preoccupation with work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is affecting things in a real way now. At first I kept telling myself it was just stress and I could manage it, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings that I’m expected to attend. Not completely avoiding everything, but enough that people notice, I think. Sometimes I just dread going into those meetings because I’m already anticipating being questioned, so I’ll delay, or I’ll keep quiet, or I’ll find reasons not to be as involved as I normally would.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it’s spilling over. My husband and I are arguing more. Not huge dramatic fights every time, but more little things turning into arguments because I’m tense or distracted. I’m also not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. Things we normally do together or just regular household stuff—I’m either too drained or my mind is still stuck on work. So yes, I’d say it’s causing problems both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors outside of work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, life is life—there are always the normal things, bills, family schedules, keeping up with the house, that sort of thing—but I haven’t had some big change outside of work that lines up with when all this started.\n\nI’ve actually thought about that, because I keep wondering if maybe I’m just overwhelmed in general and work is where it’s showing up. But nothing major happened at home, at least nothing new or dramatic. No big loss, no move, no major family crisis that I can think of. My husband and I have had more tension lately, but that feels more like it came after the work stress started, not before it.\n\nSo I’d say the new manager and the changes around how my work is being questioned are really the main thing. At least that’s the only clear stressor I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety and stress would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think it probably would, yes. I mean, I can’t say for sure because it hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so I haven’t really had that “after” period where I could see what I’m like once it’s resolved.\n\nBut my sense is that a lot of the anxiety is tied to that ongoing situation. It’s not like the issue got fixed and then months later I was still just as wound up for no reason. I haven’t had that chance, really. I’m still in it. So if the reporting situation or the way he’s handling my work were actually resolved, I do think my stress would probably come down quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop ruminating on these work interactions if you were able to find a new job or leave your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I think so… or at least I think it would give me a real chance to stop. It’s hard to know for certain, because I haven’t actually been out of the situation yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still working under the procedures he changed, so every time I start to feel like maybe I can let it go, something else happens or I have another interaction with him and I’m right back in it.\n\nSo, in my mind, finding a new job or leaving would probably remove the thing that keeps restarting the rumination. I don’t really know what I would be like after that, because I haven’t had any stretch of time where the issue was resolved and then the thoughts just kept going for months on their own. That hasn’t happened. It’s still active.\n\nI’d like to think if I weren’t having to face the same reporting structure and the same scrutiny, my mind would calm down. But I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would instantly stop,” because I haven’t had the chance to test that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the worries and stress you're experiencing would completely go away if the situation at work were to completely resolve itself tomorrow?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely, because that feels like a big promise, and I haven’t actually had the chance to see what would happen. The work situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. I mean, that’s the thing I keep coming back to—every time I think maybe I’m starting to let it go, I’m still right there in the same reporting setup, same changed procedures, same feeling that something could come up again. So it keeps getting refreshed, if that makes sense.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would all completely disappear,” because I haven’t had any period after it was resolved where these problems kept going for months on their own, or where I could compare and say, “Okay, the work issue is gone but I’m still just as stressed.” I just haven’t been there yet. So my best answer is that I think it would decrease a lot, but I don’t know if it would vanish completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to control your worry about work if you really tried to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not just sitting there deciding to worry about it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re home now, work is done, don’t keep going over it,” but then my mind goes right back to what he said in a meeting or how he questioned one of the inspection procedures I’ve been using for years.\n\nIt’s like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the way I do things, I can’t really leave it at work. I come home still wound up, and I’ll replay the conversation over and over, thinking, “Did I explain that wrong? Is he going to say I missed something? Should I have handled that quality call differently?” And these are routine calls I used to make without second-guessing myself so much.\n\nSo, could I control it if I really tried? Maybe a little, for short periods. I can distract myself sometimes, or tell myself to stop. But it doesn’t feel fully under my control right now. It keeps coming back, especially because I know I have to go in the next day and deal with the same scrutiny again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after the new operations manager began criticizing your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s how it feels to me when I look back on it. I was doing fine before he came in. Work wasn’t perfect, of course, but I wasn’t carrying it around with me like this.\n\nThen once the new operations manager started changing the procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building pretty quickly. I’d say within a few weeks I could tell I was reacting differently, like I wasn’t just annoyed in the moment — I was thinking about it afterward and feeling unsettled by it.\n\nBy maybe the second month, it was obvious to me that it wasn’t staying at work anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I had a bad meeting.” It was more like the whole situation had started following me around mentally. So yes, I do connect the start of this to when he came in and began criticizing my work and changing those procedures.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally numb or detached from things you used to enjoy since the issues at work started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as emotionally numb, exactly. I mean, I’ve heard people say that kind of thing, like they just can’t feel anything or they’re totally detached, and I don’t know that that’s really what’s happening with me.\n\nSince the work stuff started, I do think my mind is more occupied, so I may not settle into things the same way. Like if I’m trying to enjoy something, part of me might still be thinking about work, or I may not be as present as I used to be. But that feels a little different from being numb. I still care about things, and I still know what I usually enjoy. Not that every day was easy, but I wasn’t taking it home with me in the same way.\n\nOnce he started changing our procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building within weeks. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I just haven’t been myself since things changed at work. I work in inspection, and I’ve been doing my procedures a certain way for years—carefully, the way I was trained, and honestly I’ve always felt pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and she’s been picking apart the way I do things, the inspection procedures, the little calls I make day to day, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore.\n\nI come home and I’m still wound up from meetings or comments she made, and I’ll be replaying them in my head over and over—what I said, what I should’ve said, whether I looked incompetent. Then I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, which isn’t fair to him. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because I feel like I keep trying to shut it off and I just can’t.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started going over my inspection procedures and questioning things I’ve been doing for years, it’s like it follows me home. I’ll tell myself, okay, you’re not at work now, stop thinking about it, but then I’m standing in the kitchen or trying to relax and my mind is right back in the meeting. I replay what was said, what I should have said, whether I sounded defensive, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then I get wound up all over again. I do go over it in my head a lot and wonder if I should have handled certain conversations differently, or if I should have explained my process better, or maybe not sounded so defensive. So there’s some of that, like questioning myself.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure that’s the right word. I don’t feel like I’ve done something wrong on purpose or that I caused the whole situation. It’s more that when someone keeps challenging the way you do your job, especially work you’ve done for years, it makes you start wondering, “Am I missing something? Did I get too comfortable? Should I have seen this coming?” And then I can get stuck picking apart my own part in it.\n\nSo yes, I second-guess myself, but I don’t think I’m walking around feeling guilty all the time. I don’t really know what I would be like after that, because I haven’t had any stretch of time where the issue was resolved and then the thoughts just kept going for months on their own. The work situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. I mean, that’s the thing I keep coming back to—every time I think maybe I’m starting to let it go, I’m still right there in the same reporting setup, same changed procedures, same feeling that something could come up again. So my best answer is that I think it would decrease a lot, but I don’t know if it would vanish completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to control your worry about work if you really tried to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try. Not that every day was easy, but I wasn’t taking it home with me in the same way.\n\nOnce he started changing our procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building within weeks. At first I think I told myself, “Okay, this is just an adjustment, he’s new, things are changing,” but then I noticed I was reacting more strongly and thinking about it after work.\n\nBy the second month, I knew it wasn’t just ordinary work frustration anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. So yes, I would say it definitely started within three months of him beginning to criticize my work.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It feels like a lot longer sometimes, just because it’s been on my mind so much, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about two months.\n\nIt really started after the new operations manager came in and began changing my inspection procedures. That’s when things shifted for me. So, time-wise, I’d say this has been going on for around two months, not six.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

The work situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not just sitting there deciding to worry about it. Should I have handled that quality call differently?” And these are routine calls I used to make without second-guessing myself so much.\n\nSo, could I control it if I really tried? So, time-wise, I’d say this has been going on for around two months, not six.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about two months, since the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not more than six months. I know I already said the timing, but it’s been more like about two months, from when the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.

It feels longer in my head because I’ve been thinking about it so much, but if I actually count it out, it hasn’t been six months. It really started around that change at work.

Then when I’m away from the situation, I can still get pulled back into thinking about it, but I wouldn’t say I’m just sad all the time. I guess I’m having a hard time separating being stressed and disappointed from being depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop thinking about these work interactions, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because I feel like I keep trying to shut it off and I just can’t.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started going over my inspection procedures and questioning things I’ve been doing for years, it’s like it follows me home. I’ll tell myself, okay, you’re not at work now, stop thinking about it, but then I’m standing in the kitchen or trying to relax and my mind is right back in the meeting. Then at night I’m lying there going through the same conversations in my head, almost like I’m preparing to be criticized again. So there’s some of that, like questioning myself.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure that’s the right word. I don’t feel like I’ve done something wrong on purpose or that I caused the whole situation. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. So my best answer is that I think it would decrease a lot, but I don’t know if it would vanish completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to control your worry about work if you really tried to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not just sitting there deciding to worry about it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re home now, work is done, don’t keep going over it,” but then my mind goes right back to what he said in a meeting or how he questioned one of the inspection procedures I’ve been using for years.\n\nIt’s like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the way I do things, I can’t really leave it at work. I come home still wound up, and I’ll replay the conversation over and over, thinking, “Did I explain that wrong? Is he going to say I missed something? It’s more confusion and self-doubt than guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or impairment in your social or occupational functioning as a result of your preoccupation with work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is affecting things in a real way now. At first I kept telling myself it was just stress and I could manage it, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings that I’m expected to attend. Not completely avoiding everything, but enough that people notice, I think. Sometimes I just dread going into those meetings because I’m already anticipating being questioned, so I’ll delay, or I’ll keep quiet, or I’ll find reasons not to be as involved as I normally would.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it’s spilling over. My husband and I are arguing more. Not huge dramatic fights every time, but more little things turning into arguments because I’m tense or distracted. I’m also not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. Things we normally do together or just regular household stuff—I’m either too drained or my mind is still stuck on work. So yes, I’d say it’s causing problems both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors outside of work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, life is life—there are always the normal things, bills, family schedules, keeping up with the house, that sort of thing—but I haven’t had some big change outside of work that lines up with when all this started.\n\nI’ve actually thought about that, because I keep wondering if maybe I’m just overwhelmed in general and work is where it’s showing up. But nothing major happened at home, at least nothing new or dramatic. No big loss, no move, no major family crisis that I can think of. My husband and I have had more tension lately, but that feels more like it came after the work stress started, not before it.\n\nSo I’d say the new manager and the changes around how my work is being questioned are really the main thing. At least that’s the only clear stressor I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety and stress would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think it probably would, yes. I mean, I can’t say for sure because it hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so I haven’t really had that “after” period where I could see what I’m like once it’s resolved.\n\nBut my sense is that a lot of the anxiety is tied to that ongoing situation. It’s not like the issue got fixed and then months later I was still just as wound up for no reason. I haven’t had that chance, really. I’m still in it. So if the reporting situation or the way he’s handling my work were actually resolved, I do think my stress would probably come down quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop ruminating on these work interactions if you were able to find a new job or leave your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I think so… or at least I think it would give me a real chance to stop. It’s hard to know for certain, because I haven’t actually been out of the situation yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still working under the procedures he changed, so every time I start to feel like maybe I can let it go, something else happens or I have another interaction with him and I’m right back in it.\n\nSo, in my mind, finding a new job or leaving would probably remove the thing that keeps restarting the rumination. I don’t really know what I would be like after that, because I haven’t had any stretch of time where the issue was resolved and then the thoughts just kept going for months on their own. That hasn’t happened. It’s still active.\n\nI’d like to think if I weren’t having to face the same reporting structure and the same scrutiny, my mind would calm down. But I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would instantly stop,” because I haven’t had the chance to test that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the worries and stress you're experiencing would completely go away if the situation at work were to completely resolve itself tomorrow?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely, because that feels like a big promise, and I haven’t actually had the chance to see what would happen. The work situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. I mean, that’s the thing I keep coming back to—every time I think maybe I’m starting to let it go, I’m still right there in the same reporting setup, same changed procedures, same feeling that something could come up again. So it keeps getting refreshed, if that makes sense.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would all completely disappear,” because I haven’t had any period after it was resolved where these problems kept going for months on their own, or where I could compare and say, “Okay, the work issue is gone but I’m still just as stressed.” I just haven’t been there yet. So my best answer is that I think it would decrease a lot, but I don’t know if it would vanish completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to control your worry about work if you really tried to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not just sitting there deciding to worry about it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re home now, work is done, don’t keep going over it,” but then my mind goes right back to what he said in a meeting or how he questioned one of the inspection procedures I’ve been using for years.\n\nIt’s like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the way I do things, I can’t really leave it at work. I come home still wound up, and I’ll replay the conversation over and over, thinking, “Did I explain that wrong? Is he going to say I missed something? Should I have handled that quality call differently?” And these are routine calls I used to make without second-guessing myself so much.\n\nSo, could I control it if I really tried? Maybe a little, for short periods. I can distract myself sometimes, or tell myself to stop. But it doesn’t feel fully under my control right now. It keeps coming back, especially because I know I have to go in the next day and deal with the same scrutiny again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after the new operations manager began criticizing your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s how it feels to me when I look back on it. I was doing fine before he came in. Work wasn’t perfect, of course, but I wasn’t carrying it around with me like this.\n\nThen once the new operations manager started changing the procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building pretty quickly. I’d say within a few weeks I could tell I was reacting differently, like I wasn’t just annoyed in the moment — I was thinking about it afterward and feeling unsettled by it.\n\nBy maybe the second month, it was obvious to me that it wasn’t staying at work anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I had a bad meeting.” It was more like the whole situation had started following me around mentally. So yes, I do connect the start of this to when he came in and began criticizing my work and changing those procedures.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally numb or detached from things you used to enjoy since the issues at work started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as emotionally numb, exactly. I mean, I’ve heard people say that kind of thing, like they just can’t feel anything or they’re totally detached, and I don’t know that that’s really what’s happening with me.\n\nSince the work stuff started, I do think my mind is more occupied, so I may not settle into things the same way. Like if I’m trying to enjoy something, part of me might still be thinking about work, or I may not be as present as I used to be. But that feels a little different from being numb. I still care about things, and I still know what I usually enjoy. It’s more that the stress kind of crowds the space, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a real detached or numb feeling. At least not in a clear way. It’s more that I’m distracted and preoccupied sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’ve stopped feeling connected to things.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms start within 3 months of when the new operations manager began criticizing your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it did. I mean, I don’t have the exact date circled on a calendar or anything, but the timing is pretty clear in my mind. Before the new operations manager came in, I was basically doing fine with work. Not that every day was easy, but I wasn’t taking it home with me in the same way.\n\nOnce he started changing our procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building within weeks. At first I think I told myself, “Okay, this is just an adjustment, he’s new, things are changing,” but then I noticed I was reacting more strongly and thinking about it after work.\n\nBy the second month, I knew it wasn’t just ordinary work frustration anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. So yes, I would say it definitely started within three months of him beginning to criticize my work.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It feels like a lot longer sometimes, just because it’s been on my mind so much, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about two months.\n\nIt really started after the new operations manager came in and began changing my inspection procedures. That’s when things shifted for me. So, time-wise, I’d say this has been going on for around two months, not six.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. I know I already said the timing, but it’s been more like about two months, from when the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.\n\nIt feels longer in my head because I’ve been thinking about it so much, but if I actually count it out, it hasn’t been six months. It really started around that change at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I just haven’t been myself since things changed at work. I work in inspection, and I’ve been doing my procedures a certain way for years—carefully, the way I was trained, and honestly I’ve always felt pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and she’s been picking apart the way I do things, the inspection procedures, the little calls I make day to day, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore.\n\nI come home and I’m still wound up from meetings or comments she made, and I’ll be replaying them in my head over and over—what I said, what I should’ve said, whether I looked incompetent. Then I end up snapping at my husband over nothing, which isn’t fair to him. At night I lie there going back through work in my head instead of sleeping, and then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that I used to make without even thinking twice. It’s affecting me more than I want to admit, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work interactions even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s honestly one of the biggest things bothering me. It’s like I’ll be home, trying to do something normal, and then all of a sudden I’m right back in one of those meetings with the new operations manager, hearing the way she questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. If there’s an internal meeting where I know the procedures might come up, or where she might question how I made a call, I find myself trying to stay quiet or even avoiding being there if I can. At home, I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. So… not really separate from work, no. It spreads into other places, but the source still feels like work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the time since these work issues started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it sad or depressed most of the time. But it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day, every day.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated, or embarrassed, or kind of worn down by it. Then when I’m away from the situation, I can still get pulled back into thinking about it, but I wouldn’t say I’m just sad all the time. I guess I’m having a hard time separating being stressed and disappointed from being depressed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to stop thinking about these work interactions, but you can't?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because I feel like I keep trying to shut it off and I just can’t.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started going over my inspection procedures and questioning things I’ve been doing for years, it’s like it follows me home. I’ll tell myself, okay, you’re not at work now, stop thinking about it, but then I’m standing in the kitchen or trying to relax and my mind is right back in the meeting. I replay what was said, what I should have said, whether I sounded defensive, whether I missed something.\n\nAnd then I get wound up all over again. By the time I go back in the next day, I’m already tense, and then I start second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without much trouble. So yes, I do try to stop thinking about it, but it feels like my brain just keeps dragging me back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for the way things are going at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I blame myself exactly. I do go over it in my head a lot and wonder if I should have handled certain conversations differently, or if I should have explained my process better, or maybe not sounded so defensive. So there’s some of that, like questioning myself.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure that’s the right word. I don’t feel like I’ve done something wrong on purpose or that I caused the whole situation. It’s more that when someone keeps challenging the way you do your job, especially work you’ve done for years, it makes you start wondering, “Am I missing something? Did I get too comfortable? Should I have seen this coming?” And then I can get stuck picking apart my own part in it.\n\nSo yes, I second-guess myself, but I don’t think I’m walking around feeling guilty all the time. It’s more confusion and self-doubt than guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or impairment in your social or occupational functioning as a result of your preoccupation with work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is affecting things in a real way now. At first I kept telling myself it was just stress and I could manage it, but my work record has definitely taken a hit.\n\nI’ve missed some internal deadlines, which is not really like me, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings that I’m expected to attend. Not completely avoiding everything, but enough that people notice, I think. Sometimes I just dread going into those meetings because I’m already anticipating being questioned, so I’ll delay, or I’ll keep quiet, or I’ll find reasons not to be as involved as I normally would.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it’s spilling over. My husband and I are arguing more. Not huge dramatic fights every time, but more little things turning into arguments because I’m tense or distracted. I’m also not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. Things we normally do together or just regular household stuff—I’m either too drained or my mind is still stuck on work. So yes, I’d say it’s causing problems both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors outside of work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really point to, no. I mean, life is life—there are always the normal things, bills, family schedules, keeping up with the house, that sort of thing—but I haven’t had some big change outside of work that lines up with when all this started.\n\nI’ve actually thought about that, because I keep wondering if maybe I’m just overwhelmed in general and work is where it’s showing up. But nothing major happened at home, at least nothing new or dramatic. No big loss, no move, no major family crisis that I can think of. My husband and I have had more tension lately, but that feels more like it came after the work stress started, not before it.\n\nSo I’d say the new manager and the changes around how my work is being questioned are really the main thing. At least that’s the only clear stressor I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety and stress would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think it probably would, yes. I mean, I can’t say for sure because it hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so I haven’t really had that “after” period where I could see what I’m like once it’s resolved.\n\nBut my sense is that a lot of the anxiety is tied to that ongoing situation. It’s not like the issue got fixed and then months later I was still just as wound up for no reason. I haven’t had that chance, really. I’m still in it. So if the reporting situation or the way he’s handling my work were actually resolved, I do think my stress would probably come down quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop ruminating on these work interactions if you were able to find a new job or leave your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I think so… or at least I think it would give me a real chance to stop. It’s hard to know for certain, because I haven’t actually been out of the situation yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still working under the procedures he changed, so every time I start to feel like maybe I can let it go, something else happens or I have another interaction with him and I’m right back in it.\n\nSo, in my mind, finding a new job or leaving would probably remove the thing that keeps restarting the rumination. I don’t really know what I would be like after that, because I haven’t had any stretch of time where the issue was resolved and then the thoughts just kept going for months on their own. That hasn’t happened. It’s still active.\n\nI’d like to think if I weren’t having to face the same reporting structure and the same scrutiny, my mind would calm down. But I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would instantly stop,” because I haven’t had the chance to test that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the worries and stress you're experiencing would completely go away if the situation at work were to completely resolve itself tomorrow?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely, because that feels like a big promise, and I haven’t actually had the chance to see what would happen. The work situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s not like there’s been a clean break where everything was fixed and then I could see whether my mind settled down or whether I kept carrying it.\n\nI do think a lot of it would probably ease if it resolved tomorrow. I mean, that’s the thing I keep coming back to—every time I think maybe I’m starting to let it go, I’m still right there in the same reporting setup, same changed procedures, same feeling that something could come up again. So it keeps getting refreshed, if that makes sense.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, it would all completely disappear,” because I haven’t had any period after it was resolved where these problems kept going for months on their own, or where I could compare and say, “Okay, the work issue is gone but I’m still just as stressed.” I just haven’t been there yet. So my best answer is that I think it would decrease a lot, but I don’t know if it would vanish completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to control your worry about work if you really tried to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not just sitting there deciding to worry about it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re home now, work is done, don’t keep going over it,” but then my mind goes right back to what he said in a meeting or how he questioned one of the inspection procedures I’ve been using for years.\n\nIt’s like ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the way I do things, I can’t really leave it at work. I come home still wound up, and I’ll replay the conversation over and over, thinking, “Did I explain that wrong? Is he going to say I missed something? Should I have handled that quality call differently?” And these are routine calls I used to make without second-guessing myself so much.\n\nSo, could I control it if I really tried? Maybe a little, for short periods. I can distract myself sometimes, or tell myself to stop. But it doesn’t feel fully under my control right now. It keeps coming back, especially because I know I have to go in the next day and deal with the same scrutiny again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started after the new operations manager began criticizing your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s how it feels to me when I look back on it. I was doing fine before he came in. Work wasn’t perfect, of course, but I wasn’t carrying it around with me like this.\n\nThen once the new operations manager started changing the procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building pretty quickly. I’d say within a few weeks I could tell I was reacting differently, like I wasn’t just annoyed in the moment — I was thinking about it afterward and feeling unsettled by it.\n\nBy maybe the second month, it was obvious to me that it wasn’t staying at work anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. It wasn’t just, “Oh, I had a bad meeting.” It was more like the whole situation had started following me around mentally. So yes, I do connect the start of this to when he came in and began criticizing my work and changing those procedures.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally numb or detached from things you used to enjoy since the issues at work started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as emotionally numb, exactly. I mean, I’ve heard people say that kind of thing, like they just can’t feel anything or they’re totally detached, and I don’t know that that’s really what’s happening with me.\n\nSince the work stuff started, I do think my mind is more occupied, so I may not settle into things the same way. Like if I’m trying to enjoy something, part of me might still be thinking about work, or I may not be as present as I used to be. But that feels a little different from being numb. I still care about things, and I still know what I usually enjoy. It’s more that the stress kind of crowds the space, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a real detached or numb feeling. At least not in a clear way. It’s more that I’m distracted and preoccupied sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’ve stopped feeling connected to things.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms start within 3 months of when the new operations manager began criticizing your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it did. I mean, I don’t have the exact date circled on a calendar or anything, but the timing is pretty clear in my mind. Before the new operations manager came in, I was basically doing fine with work. Not that every day was easy, but I wasn’t taking it home with me in the same way.\n\nOnce he started changing our procedures and criticizing how I was doing things, it started building within weeks. At first I think I told myself, “Okay, this is just an adjustment, he’s new, things are changing,” but then I noticed I was reacting more strongly and thinking about it after work.\n\nBy the second month, I knew it wasn’t just ordinary work frustration anymore. My reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday. So yes, I would say it definitely started within three months of him beginning to criticize my work.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It feels like a lot longer sometimes, just because it’s been on my mind so much, but if I’m counting it out, it’s been about two months.\n\nIt really started after the new operations manager came in and began changing my inspection procedures. That’s when things shifted for me. So, time-wise, I’d say this has been going on for around two months, not six.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than 6 months since they started?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. I know I already said the timing, but it’s been more like about two months, from when the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.\n\nIt feels longer in my head because I’ve been thinking about it so much, but if I actually count it out, it hasn’t been six months. It really started around that change at work.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for less than 6 months since they started, or have they continued beyond that point?\n\nPatient: Less than six months, definitely. That was the point where things shifted for me. So no, it hasn’t continued beyond six months — it’s more like two months since it started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
